#  ========== Webscrapping the data using alpha_vantage library ==========

api_key = 'YOUR API KEY HERE'

url = (
    "https://www.alphavantage.co/query?"
    "function=FX_DAILY&"
    "from_symbol=EUR&"
    "to_symbol=USD&"
    "outputsize=full&"
    f"apikey={api_key}"
)

response = requests.get(url)
data = response.json()

if "Time Series FX (Daily)" not in data:
    raise ValueError(data)

df = pd.DataFrame.from_dict(
    data["Time Series FX (Daily)"],
    orient="index"
)

df.index = pd.to_datetime(df.index)
df = df.sort_index().astype(float)

# =========================
# VOLATILITY (Parkinson)
# =========================

df["rv"] = (1 / (4 * np.log(2))) * (
    np.log(df["2. high"] / df["3. low"]) ** 2
)

# =========================
# VOLUME Proxy 
# =========================

df["volume_proxy"] = (df["2. high"] - df["3. low"]) / df["4. close"]

# =========================
# FILTER
# =========================

df = df.loc["2024-09-16":"2026-09-16"]

df = df.rename(columns={
    '1. open': 'Open',
    '2. high': 'High',
    '3. low': 'Low',
    '4. close': 'Close'
})

df['close'] = df['Close']
df['high']  = df['High']
df['low']   = df['Low']

# =========================
# VOL REGIME
# =========================

vol_q_low  = df['rv'].quantile(0.3)
vol_q_high = df['rv'].quantile(0.7)

def classify_zone_strength(z):
    vol_at_touch = df['rv'].iloc[z['last_touch']]
    if vol_at_touch < vol_q_low:
        return 'strong'
    elif vol_at_touch > vol_q_high:
        return 'weak'
    else:
        return 'medium'

# =========================
# DOM FOREX CORE
# =========================

def compute_atr(df, period=14):
    tr = pd.concat([
        df['high'] - df['low'],
        (df['high'] - df['close'].shift()).abs(),
        (df['low'] - df['close'].shift()).abs()
    ], axis=1).max(axis=1)
    return tr.rolling(period).mean()

def detect_pivots(df, window=10):
    pivots = []
    for i in range(window, len(df)-window):
        if df['high'].iloc[i] == df['high'].iloc[i-window:i+window].max():
            pivots.append((i, df['high'].iloc[i]))
        elif df['low'].iloc[i] == df['low'].iloc[i-window:i+window].min():
            pivots.append((i, df['low'].iloc[i]))
    return pivots

def cluster_levels(pivots, atr, atr_mult=0.6):
    clusters = []
    for idx, price in pivots:
        tol = atr.iloc[idx] * atr_mult
        for c in clusters:
            if abs(price - c['mean']) < tol:
                c['prices'].append(price)
                c['indices'].append(idx)
                c['mean'] = np.mean(c['prices'])
                break
        else:
            clusters.append({
                'prices': [price],
                'indices': [idx],
                'mean': price
            })
    return clusters

def compute_strength(cluster, current_idx):
    touches = len(cluster['prices'])
    recency = sum(np.exp(-0.01*(current_idx-i)) for i in cluster['indices'])
    return touches + recency

def build_zones(df):
    atr = compute_atr(df)
    pivots = detect_pivots(df)
    clusters = cluster_levels(pivots, atr)
    current_idx = len(df)-1
    zones = []
    for c in clusters:
        strength = compute_strength(c, current_idx)
        if strength < 2:
            continue
        zone = {
            'low': min(c['prices']),
            'high': max(c['prices']),
            'mid': c['mean'],
            'strength': strength,
            'last_touch': max(c['indices'])
        }
        zone['class'] = classify_zone_strength(zone)
        zones.append(zone)
    return zones

zones = build_zones(df)

supports = [z for z in zones if z['mid'] < df['close'].iloc[-1]]
resistances = [z for z in zones if z['mid'] >= df['close'].iloc[-1]]


# =========================================================
# GENERIC INSTITUTIONAL FILTER (SUPPORTS and RESISTANCES)
# =========================================================

def is_institutional_zone(zone, df, round_step=0.0050, pip_tolerance=0.0010):
    """
    Evaluates whether a zone (Support or Resistance) meets institutional criteria:
    1. Proximity to a Key Level (Big Figure: 1.1400, 1.1450, 1.1500, etc.)
    2. Presence of Absorption / High Volume Proxy at the last touch
    """
    mid_price = zone['mid']       
    # 1. Key Level (Big Figure) Criterion: Proximity to round numbers
    nearest_key_level = np.round(mid_price / round_step) * round_step
    is_key_level = abs(mid_price - nearest_key_level) <= pip_tolerance       
    # 2. Institutional Absorption Criterion (Last-Touch Volume Proxy)
    last_touch_idx = zone['last_touch']
    vol_proxy = df['volume_proxy'].iloc[last_touch_idx]
    avg_vol_proxy = df['volume_proxy'].rolling(20).mean().iloc[last_touch_idx]   
    has_absorption = vol_proxy > 1.25 * avg_vol_proxy       
    # 3. Classification
    if is_key_level and has_absorption:
        return True, 'INSTITUTIONAL_STRONG'
    elif is_key_level or has_absorption:
        return True, 'INSTITUTIONAL_MEDIUM'
    else:
        return False, 'RETAIL_ZONE'


# 1. Processing Institutional Supports
inst_supports = []
for s in supports:
    is_inst, inst_type = is_institutional_zone(s, df)
    s['is_institutional'] = is_inst
    s['inst_type'] = inst_type
    if is_inst:
        inst_supports.append(s)

# 2. Navigating Institutional Resistance
inst_resistances = []
for r in resistances:
    is_inst, inst_type = is_institutional_zone(r, df)
    r['is_institutional'] = is_inst
    r['inst_type'] = inst_type
    if is_inst:
        inst_resistances.append(r)


print("--- FILTERED INSTITUTIONAL SUPPORT LEVELS ---")
for s in inst_supports:
    print(f"Mid: {s['mid']:.5f} | Type: {s['inst_type']} | Class: {s['class']} | Force: {s['strength']:.2f}")

#  Mid: 1.07468 | Type: INSTITUTIONAL_MEDIUM | Class: weak | Force: 2.03
#  Mid: 1.09458 | Type: INSTITUTIONAL_MEDIUM | Class: medium | Force: 2.03
#  Mid: 1.03460 | Type: INSTITUTIONAL_MEDIUM | Class: medium | Force: 2.03
#  Mid: 1.01938 | Type: INSTITUTIONAL_STRONG | Class: weak | Force: 2.03
#  Mid: 1.14030 | Type: INSTITUTIONAL_STRONG | Class: weak | Force: 3.59

print("\n--- FILTERED INSTITUTIONAL RESISTANCES ---")
for r in inst_resistances:
    print(f"Mid: {r['mid']:.5f} | Type: {r['inst_type']} | Class: {r['class']} | Force: {r['strength']:.2f}")

#  Mid: 1.18136 | Type: INSTITUTIONAL_MEDIUM | Class: weak | Force: 5.97
#  Mid: 1.14793 | Type: INSTITUTIONAL_MEDIUM | Class: medium | Force: 3.86

fig, (ax1, ax2) = plt.subplots(

current_price = df['Close'].iloc[-1]
T = len(df)

def plot_zone(z, base_color):
    age = T - z['last_touch']
    strength_norm = min(1.0, z['strength'] / 10)
    if z['class'] == 'strong':
        alpha = 0.35 + 0.4 * strength_norm
        linewidth = 2.2
    elif z['class'] == 'medium':
        alpha = 0.20 + 0.3 * strength_norm
        linewidth = 1.6
    else:
        alpha = 0.10 + 0.2 * strength_norm
        linewidth = 1.0
    decay = np.exp(-0.002 * age)
    alpha *= decay
    zone_color = base_color
    if z['low'] <= current_price <= z['high']:
        zone_color = 'yellow'
        alpha = 0.65
        linewidth = 2.8
    ax1.axhspan(z['low'], z['high'], alpha=alpha, color=zone_color)
    ax1.hlines(
        z['mid'],
        xmin=df.index[0],
        xmax=df.index[-1],
        linestyles='dashed',
        linewidth=linewidth,
        alpha=alpha
    )

for z in supports:
    plot_zone(z, 'blue')

for z in resistances:
    plot_zone(z, 'red')

ax1.set_title('EURUSD + Adaptive DOM Forex Zones')
ax1.set_ylabel('Price')
ax1.grid(True)

#  Figure_1.1a(Data Presentation) 

def get_structural_event_dummies(df_target, horizon=1):
    """
    Generates dummy variables for high-impact events (Impact = 3) and holidays
    in the Eurozone and the US, using a calendar of exact dates to avoid false positives.
    """
    df_hist = df_target.copy()
    #	
    # Extension of the Index to the Out-of-Sample Horizon
	#
    last_date = df_hist.index[-1]
    future_days = [last_date + BDay(i) for i in range(1, horizon + 1)]
    full_index = df_hist.index.append(pd.DatetimeIndex(future_days))
    #	
    # Initializes the signals DataFrame.
	#
    full_period = pd.DataFrame(index=full_index)
    full_period['news_usa'] = 0
    full_period['news_eur'] = 0
    full_period['news_geo'] = 0
    full_period['holiday_usa'] = 0
    full_period['holiday_eur'] = 0
    #
    idx = full_period.index
    iso_week = idx.isocalendar().week
    #
    # =========================================================================
    # 2. BANK HOLIDAYS (USA & EUROZONE)
    # =========================================================================
    years = list(range(idx[0].year, idx[-1].year + 1))
    us_dates = set(holidays.US(years=years).keys())
    ecb_dates = set(holidays.EuropeanCentralBank(years=years).keys())
    #
    idx_as_dates = [d.date() for d in idx]
    full_period['holiday_usa'] = [1 if d in us_dates else 0 for d in idx_as_dates]
    full_period['holiday_eur'] = [1 if d in ecb_dates else 0 for d in idx_as_dates]
    #
    # =========================================================================
    # 3. EXACT DATES - HIGH-IMPACT EVENTS (USA)
    # =========================================================================
    # NFP: First Friday of the month (Exact rule)
    nfp_mask = (idx.dayofweek == 4) & (idx.day <= 7)
    #
    # FOMC, US CPI, and Retail Sales (Official dates released)
    usa_exact_dates = pd.to_datetime([
        # --- 2025 ---
        '2025-01-15', '2025-01-16', '2025-01-29', '2025-02-12', '2025-02-14',
        '2025-03-12', '2025-03-17', '2025-03-19', '2025-04-10', '2025-04-16',
        '2025-05-07', '2025-05-13', '2025-05-15', '2025-06-11', '2025-06-17',
        '2025-06-18', '2025-07-15', '2025-07-16', '2025-07-30', '2025-08-13',
        '2025-08-15', '2025-09-16', '2025-09-17', '2025-10-15', '2025-10-16',
        '2025-10-29', '2025-11-13', '2025-11-14', '2025-12-10', '2025-12-16',
        # --- 2026 ---
        '2026-01-14', '2026-01-16', '2026-01-28', '2026-02-11', '2026-02-13',
        '2026-03-11', '2026-03-16', '2026-03-18', '2026-04-14', '2026-04-16',
        '2026-05-12', '2026-05-15', '2026-06-10', '2026-06-16', '2026-06-17',
        '2026-07-14', '2026-07-16', '2026-07-29', '2026-08-12', '2026-08-14',
        '2026-09-15', '2026-09-16', # 15/09 (Retail Sales) e 16/09 (FOMC Rate Decision)
        '2026-10-14', '2026-10-16', '2026-10-28', '2026-11-12', '2026-11-13',
        '2026-12-09', '2026-12-15'
    ])    
    # Creating an isolated mask for critical events (FOMC / CPI / Retail)
    fomc_critical_mask = idx.normalize().isin(usa_exact_dates)    
    full_period.loc[nfp_mask | fomc_critical_mask, 'news_usa'] = 1    
    # =========================================================================
    # 4. EXACT DATES - HIGH-IMPACT EVENTS (EUR)
    # =========================================================================
    eur_exact_dates = pd.to_datetime([
        # ECB Interest Rate Decisions (Thursdays)
        # --- 2025 ---
        '2025-01-30', '2025-03-06', '2025-04-17', '2025-06-05', 
        '2025-07-24', '2025-09-11', '2025-10-30', '2025-12-18',
        # --- 2026 ---
        '2026-02-05', '2026-03-19', '2026-04-30', '2026-06-11', 
        '2026-07-23', '2026-09-10', '2026-10-29', '2026-12-17'
    ])   
    # Eurozone Flash PMIs (21st to 23rd business day)
    pmi_eur = (idx.day >= 21) & (idx.day <= 23) & (idx.dayofweek <= 4)
    # Eurozone Preliminary CPI (Last day or the 1st)
    cpi_eur = ((idx.day >= 29) | (idx.day <= 1)) & (idx.dayofweek <= 4)
    #   
    eur_exact_mask = idx.normalize().isin(eur_exact_dates)
    full_period.loc[pmi_eur | cpi_eur | eur_exact_mask, 'news_eur'] = 1    
    # =========================================================================
    # 5. GEOPOLITICAL / STRUCTURAL EVENTS
    # =========================================================================
    jackson_hole = (idx.month == 8) & (idx.day >= 22) & (idx.day <= 28) & (idx.dayofweek.isin([3, 4]))
    davos = (idx.month == 1) & (iso_week == 3) & (idx.dayofweek <= 4)
    full_period.loc[jackson_hole | davos, 'news_geo'] = 1
    #   
    # Suppression of secondary news on bank holidays (EXCEPT critical FOMC / NFP)
    full_period.loc[(full_period['holiday_usa'] == 1) & (~fomc_critical_mask), 'news_usa'] = 0
    full_period.loc[(full_period['holiday_eur'] == 1) & (~eur_exact_mask), 'news_eur'] = 0
    #
    # =========================================================================
    # 6. OUTPUT AND UNIFICATION DICTIONARY
    # =========================================================================
    news_next = {}
    for i, d in enumerate(future_days, 1):
        news_next[f'horizon_{i}'] = {
            'date': d.strftime('%Y-%m-%d'),
            'news_usa': int(full_period.loc[d, 'news_usa']),
            'news_eur': int(full_period.loc[d, 'news_eur']),
            'news_geo': int(full_period.loc[d, 'news_geo']),
            'holiday_usa': int(full_period.loc[d, 'holiday_usa']),
            'holiday_eur': int(full_period.loc[d, 'holiday_eur'])
        }    
    #
    dummy_cols = ['news_usa', 'news_eur', 'news_geo', 'holiday_usa', 'holiday_eur']
    df_historical = df_hist.join(full_period.loc[df_hist.index, dummy_cols])
    #
    return df_historical, news_next
	
	
df_filtered , news_next  = get_structural_event_dummies(df)

#########################*/                                    *\#########################
#
#    
#    Time Series Stationarity and Fractional Differencing:
#
#    Many macroeconomic indicators exhibit an expocurrential trend due to
#    their linkage with expocurrential population growth. To apply linear
#    modeling methods, achieving stationarity is crucial. The standard
#    process typically involves:
#
#       1. Take logarithms: This transforms the series to exhibit a linear trend.
#       2. Take the difference: This step is then applied to achieve a stationary series.
#
#    Reference:
#    COLE, T. J.; ALTMAN, D. G. Statistics Notes: Percentage differences, symmetry,
#    and natural logarithms. BMJ, v. 358, ago., p. j3683, 2017.
#    Available at: https://doi.org/20.1136/bmj.j3683. Accessed on: 19 Jun. 2025.
#
#    This module specifically focuses on **fractional differencing** of time
#    series data. Its primary goal is to remove trends and achieve stationarity
#    while, importantly, preserving the "memory" of the series. This memory
#    preservation is vital for effective predictive modeling, particularly
#    in domains like finance.
#
#    The underlying principle of fractional differencing is based on the series
#    expansion of the differencing operator, commonly represented as (1-L)^d,
#    where 'L' is the lag operator and 'd' denotes the fractional differentiation order.
#
#    Reference:
#    DE PRADO, Marcos López. Advances in Financial Machine Learning. 1st ed. Hoboken: Wiley, 2017.
# 
#
#########################*/                                    *\#########################



def getWeights(d,lags):
     """
       Calculates the weights (coefficients) for the fractional differencing operator.
       These weights are derived from the series expansion of (1 - L)^d, where 'L' is the
	   lag operator and 'd' is the fractional differencing order.
	 Args:
        d (float): The fractional differencing order. Can be any real number.
        lags (int): The number of coefficients to compute. Represents the depth
                    of the look-back window for the differencing operation.

     Returns:
        numpy.ndarray: A 2D NumPy array of shape (lags, 1) containing the
                       fractional differencing weights.
	 """
     w=[1] # Initialize with the first weight (coefficient of current observation)
     for k in range(1,lags):
	     # Calculate subsequent weights using the recursive formula:
		 # w_k = -w_{k-1} * (d - k + 1) / k		 
         w.append(-w[-1]*((d-k+1))/k)
	 # Reshape to a column vector for consistency with common linear algebra operations	 
     w=np.array(w).reshape(-1,1)
     return w
	 


def plotWeights(dRange, lags, numberPlots):
     """
     Plots the lag coefficients (weights) for various orders of fractional differencing.

     This visualization helps understand how different 'd' values impact the weights,
     especially their decay over increasing lags.

     Args:
        dRange (tuple): A tuple (start_d, end_d) defining the range of
                        fractional differencing orders to plot.
        lags (int): The maximum number of lag coefficients to plot for each 'd'.
        numberPlots (int): The number of distinct 'd' values to evenly sample
                           within dRange for plotting.
     """
	 # Initialize a DataFrame to store weights for different 'd' values
     weights=pd.DataFrame(np.zeros((lags, numberPlots)))
	 # Generate evenly spaced 'd' values within the specified range
     interval=np.linspace(dRange[0],dRange[1],numberPlots)
	 # Compute weights for each sampled 'd' value
     for i, diff_order in enumerate(interval):
         weights[i]=getWeights(diff_order,lags)
     # Set DataFrame column names to the corresponding 'd' values for clarity in the plot legend
	 #
     weights.columns = [round(x,2) for x in interval]
	 #
	 # Generate the plot
     fig=weights.plot(figsize=(15,6))
	 #
	 # Customize plot labels and title for better readability
     plt.legend(title='Order of differencing')
     plt.title('Lag coefficients for various orders of differencing')
     plt.xlabel('lag coefficients')
     plt.grid(False)
     plt.show()
	 


	 
def cutoff_find(order,cutoff,start_lags): 
     """
     Finds the minimum number of lags required for the absolute value of the
     last fractional differencing weight to fall below a specified cutoff.

     This function helps determine an appropriate 'memory cutoff' for the
     fractional differencing operation, ensuring that coefficients
     beyond a certain point are negligible.

     Args:
        order (float): The fractional differencing order (our 'd').
        cutoff (float): The threshold value. The function stops when the
                        absolute value of the last weight is less than or
                        equal to this cutoff. (e.g., 1e-5)
        start_lags (int, optional): An initial number of lags to start the search from.
                                     Can be set to a higher value to potentially speed
                                     up the function if a large number of lags is expected.
                                     Defaults to 1.
     Returns:
        int: The minimum number of lags at which the absolute value of the
             last computed weight falls below or equals the cutoff.
     """
     val=np.inf
     lags=start_lags
     while abs(val)>cutoff:
         w=getWeights(order, lags)
         val=w[len(w)-1]
         lags+=1
     return lags


	 
#########################*/                                    *\#########################
#
#                        ==== Fractional Differencing Considerations ====
#    Larger series may allow for a lower cutoff to preserve memory in the periodogram.
#   Generally, the higher the differentiation order 'd', the shorter the series becomes.
#   In this specific case, the cutoff, here referred to as 'tau', was set to 1e-2.
#
#########################*/                                    *\#########################
	 
	 
def ts_differencing_tau(series, order, tau):
     """
     Applies fractional differencing to a pandas Series using a cutoff
     determined by 'tau' (a significance threshold for the weights).
     This function performs the actual fractional differencing operation,
     effectively creating a new series that is approximately stationary
     while retaining long-term memory properties based on the chosen 'd'
     and 'tau' parameters.
     Args:
        series (pd.Series): The input time series to be fractionally differenced.
                            Expected to be a numerical pandas Series.
        order (float): The fractional differencing order (d).
        tau (float): The cutoff threshold for the weights. The number of lags
                     used in the differencing will be determined such that
                     the last weight is below this 'tau'.
     Returns:
        pd.Series: The fractionally differenced time series. The initial
                   `lag_cutoff` number of NaNs (or zeros if fillna(0) is used)
                   are removed, as these cannot be computed due to insufficient
                   past data.
     """
     lag_cutoff=(cutoff_find(order,tau,1))  # Find the required number of lags based on the specified order and tau cutoff
     weights=getWeights(order, lag_cutoff)  # Get the weights for the determined number of lags
	 
     res=0  # Initialize the result series by accumulating the weighted sum of lagged series
     for k in range(lag_cutoff):  #  Apply the weighted sum of lagged series
         res += weights[k]*series.shift(k).fillna(0) # iterates through the weights and shifts the series accordingly
     return res[lag_cutoff:]
	 


#########################*/                                    *\#########################
#            
#                       ==== Parameters for Fractional Differencing Optimization ====
#
#    Define a range of possible fractional differencing orders 'd'.
#    This creates a sequence from 1/200 (0.005) to 199/200 (0.995) in increments of 0.005.
#    This range typically covers values between 0 (original series) and 1 (integer differencing).
# 
#########################*/                                    *\#########################

possible_d=np.divide(range(1,200),200)

# Define 'tau' (cutoff for weights). This threshold determines the number of lags
# used in the fractional differencing calculation. Weights smaller than 'tau' are
# considered negligible, effectively truncating the memory window.
# As discussed, a larger series might allow for a lower 'tau' to preserve more memory.
# In this specific case, 'tau' is set to 1e-2 (0.01).

tau=1e-2

# Initialize a list to store the p-values from the Augmented Dickey-Fuller (ADF) test.
# The ADF test is used to check for stationarity of the differenced series.
# A lower p-value (typically < 0.05 or < 0.01) indicates stronger evidence against
# the null hypothesis of non-stationarity (i.e., the series is stationary).

log_adf_stat_holder=[None]*len(possible_d)

# ==== Iterative Fractional Differencing and Stationarity Testing ====

# Loop through each possible 'd' value to find the optimal order.
# The goal is to find the smallest 'd' that results in a stationary series (low ADF p-value).


for i in range(len(possible_d)):
    current_d_order = possible_d[i]	
	# 1. Apply natural logarithm transformation to the 'Close' prices of EURUSD.
    #    This step aims to linearize any expocurrential trends and stabilize variance.
    log_series = np.log(pd.DataFrame(EURUSD["Close"])) 	
	# 2. Apply fractional differencing using the current 'd' order and 'tau' cutoff.
    #    This returns a new series that is potentially stationary.
    differenced_series = ts_differencing_tau(log_series, current_d_order, tau)
    # 3. Perform the Augmented Dickey-Fuller (ADF) test on the differenced series.
    #    adfuller() returns several values; the [1] index retrieves the p-value.
    log_adf_stat_holder[i] = adfuller(differenced_series)[1]
	
    
# ==== Visualization of Stationarity vs. Differencing Order ====

# Plotting the p-values from the ADF test against the corresponding 'd' values.
# This plot visually helps identify the minimum 'd' order at which the series
# becomes stationary (i.e., where the p-value drops below a significance level).
plt.plot(possible_d, log_adf_stat_holder)

# Add a horizontal red line representing a common significance level (alpha).
# For ADF test, p-values below this line indicate rejection of the null hypothesis
# of non-stationarity. 
plt.axhline(y=0.05, color='r', linestyle='--', label='ADF P-value Threshold')


# Customize plot labels for clarity.
plt.xlabel('Fractional Differencing Order (d)') 
plt.ylabel('ADF Test P-value for Log-Differenced EURUSD Close Price')
plt.title('ADF Test P-values vs. Fractional Differencing Order for EURUSD') 
plt.legend() 
plt.grid(True) 
plt.show()

# After running this, you would typically inspect the plot to find the smallest 'd'
# for which the p-value consistently stays below your chosen significance threshold.
# This 'd' value is then considered the optimal fractional differencing order
# for your time series, balancing stationarity with memory preservation.

# Figura1.1b_(ADF Test P-values vs. Fractional Differencing Order for EURUSD)

d=1.00
EURUSD_Close_fractional_difference  = (ts_differencing_tau(np.log(EURUSD["Close"]), d,tau )) 
EURUSD_Close_fractional_difference = EURUSD_Close_fractional_difference.fillna(method='ffill')  
pd.DataFrame(EURUSD_Close_fractional_difference).to_csv(r'C:\Users\Rafael Xavier Botelho\Documents\FOREX_EURSUD\EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference.csv', index=None)
EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference = pd.read_csv("C:/Users/Rafael Xavier Botelho/Documents/FOREX_EURSUD/EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference.csv")

def nan_helper(y):
    """
    Helper function to locate NaNs and provide an interpolation utility.

    This function is designed to assist in cleaning time series data by identifying
    missing values (NaNs) and providing a mechanism to interpolate them,
    commonly used for linear interpolation.

    Args:
        y (np.ndarray or pd.Series): A 1D array or Series that may contain NaNs.

    Returns:
        tuple: A tuple containing:
            - nans (np.ndarray or pd.Series of bool): A boolean array/Series where
              True indicates NaN values.
            - x (function): A lambda function that, when called with a boolean
              array, returns the indices of the True values. This is typically
              used to convert NaN indices to interpolated values.

    Example:
        # Linear interpolation of NaNs
        # (Assuming 'y' is your original data with NaNs)
        nans, x = nan_helper(y)
        y[nans] = np.interp(x(nans), x(~nans), y[~nans])
    """
    return np.isnan(y), lambda z: z.nonzero()[0]


nans, x= nan_helper(EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference.values)
EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference.values[nans]= np.interp(x(nans), x(~nans), EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference.values[~nans])

def get_top_periodic_compocurrents(series, top_n=3):
    """
    Estimate the power spectral density (PSD) using a periodogram,
    to identify the top N dominant frequencies (excluding zero),
    and return their corresponding periods and power levels.

    Parameters:
        series (array-like): 1D time series data
        top_n (int): Number of dominant compocurrents to return (default is 3)

    Returns:
        dict: Dictionary containing top N periods, frequencies, and powers
    """
    # Ensure 1D input
    series = np.squeeze(series)
	#
    # Compute the periodogram (frequency and power)
	#
    f, Pxx = signal.periodogram(series)
	#
    # Exclude the zero frequency to avoid division by zero (inf periods)
	#
    nonzero_mask = f > 0
    f = f[nonzero_mask]
    Pxx = Pxx[nonzero_mask]
	#
    # Get indices of top N power peaks
	#
    top_indices = np.flip(np.argsort(Pxx))[:top_n]
	#
    # Extract corresponding frequencies, powers, and compute periods
	#
    top_freqs = f[top_indices]
    top_power = Pxx[top_indices]
    top_periods = 1 / top_freqs
	#
    # Build result dictionary
	#
    top_compocurrents = {}
    for i in range(top_n):
        top_compocurrents[f'period{i+1}'] = top_periods[i]
        top_compocurrents[f'freq{i+1}'] = top_freqs[i]
        top_compocurrents[f'power{i+1}'] = top_power[i]
    return top_compocurrents, Pxx ,f 

# Get top 3 cyclical compocurrents
top_cycles ,Pxx, f = get_top_periodic_compocurrents(EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference.squeeze())

print(top_cycles)

#    'period3': 2.3378378378378377, 'freq3': 0.4277456647398844, 'power3': 0.00020226375534358}

# Peak 1, 2 and 3 (period ~ 5.64, 2.78, 2.34 samples respectively )  correspond to short-term fluctuations.
# These may arise from short-memory dynamics, filtering artifacts, or microstructure effects

# The spectrum does not exhibit a single dominant peak, suggesting the filtered series
# is largely broadband with no strong periodic structure.

def fisher_test(psd):
    """
    Fisher's g-test for detecting a dominant periodic compocurrent
    in a power spectral density (PSD).

    The test checks whether the largest peak in the spectrum is
    significantly larger than expected under the null hypothesis
    of white noise.

    Parameters
    ========--
    psd : array-like
        Vector containing the Power Spectral Density (PSD),
        typically obtained from methods such as:
            - scipy.signal.periodogram
            - scipy.signal.welch
            - multitaper PSD

    Returns
    ========
    g : float
        Fisher's statistic.
        Measures the proportion of the total spectral energy
        concentrated in the largest peak.

        g = max(PSD) / sum(PSD)

        The larger g is, the stronger the evidence of a dominant cycle.

    p_value : float
        Approximate p-value of the test.
        Measures the probability of observing a spectral peak as large
        as the observed one if the series were only white noise.

        small p -> significant cycle
        large p -> peak can be explained by noise.
    """
    # g statistic: ratio between the largest spectral peak
    # and the total spectral energy
    g = np.max(psd) / np.sum(psd)
    # Number of analyzed frequencies
    N = len(psd)
    # Approximate p-value of the g statistic
    # under the null hypothesis of white noise
    p_value = 1 - (1 - g)**(N - 1)
    return g, p_value
	
g, p = fisher_test(Pxx)

print("Fisher statistic:", g)

# Fisher statistic:  0.020242411976523607

#  The highest peak in the spectrum represents only ~2.00% of the total spectral energy,
#  thus being  very dispersed with  no clear dominant frequency.

# | g         | interpretation     |
# | ========- | ================-- |
# | >0.1      | stong cycle        |
# | 0.05–0.1  | moderate cycle     |
# | 0.02–0.05 | weak cycle         |
# | <0.02     | noisy              |

print("p-value:", p)
# p-value: 0.9948877979881088

#  This value measures the probability of observing such a large peak 
#  even if the series is white noise.	

#  | p-value  | interpretation                 |
#  | ======== | ============================-  |
#  | <0.01    | highly significant cycle       |
#  | <0.05    | significant cycle              |
#  | 0.05–0.1 | weak evidence                  |
#  | >0.1     | not significant                |

#  Detection of Long Memory in the Spectrum 
mask = f > 0
f = f[mask]
Pxx = Pxx[mask]

# evitar log(0)
Pxx = Pxx + 1e-12

logf = np.log(f)
logS = np.log(Pxx)

# remove inf/nan
mask = np.isfinite(logf) & np.isfinite(logS)

coef = np.polyfit(logf, logS, 1)

d_est = -coef[0] / 2

time_trim = len(EURUSD_Dates) - len(EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference)

    EURSUD_Close_fractional_difference.values.squeeze(),

# ==============================================================================

# Absolute 95th percentile threshold (magnitude)
threshold_eva = float(abs_returns.quantile(0.95))

# Raw peaks above the threshold
raw_extremes = returns[abs_returns > threshold_eva]

# Manual De-clustering (5-day window to respect the i.i.d. assumption)
declustered_extremes = []
last_date = None
min_gap_days = 5

for date, val in raw_extremes.items():
    if last_date is None:
        declustered_extremes.append((date, val))
        last_date = date
    else:
        # Handles the difference depending on the index type.
        gap = (date - last_date).days if hasattr((date - last_date), 'days') else (date - last_date)        
        if gap >= min_gap_days:
            declustered_extremes.append((date, val))
            last_date = date

extremes = pd.Series(
    data=[x[1] for x in declustered_extremes],
    index=[x[0] for x in declustered_extremes]
)

# Excesses over the threshold (u)
exceedances = extremes.abs() - threshold_eva

# ==============================================================================
# 3. FREQUENCY AND INTERVAL ANALYSIS (PRECISE METRICS)
# ==============================================================================

n_extremes = len(extremes)
mean_excess = exceedances.mean()
median_excess = exceedances.median()

# Time interval in candles/days (without the .dt suffix)
interarrival = pd.Series(extremes.index).diff().dropna()

mean_gap = interarrival.mean() if len(interarrival) > 0 else 0
min_gap = interarrival.min() if len(interarrival) > 0 else 0

mean_gap_days = mean_gap.total_seconds() / 86400 # From seconds to days 
min_gap_days = min_gap.total_seconds() / 86400

total_days = len(returns)
freq_per_day = n_extremes / total_days

print("===== FREQUENCY ANALYSIS (DECLUSTERED) =====")
print(f"Total trading days:           {total_days}")
#  Total trading days:           519
print(f"Independent peaks count:      {n_extremes}")
#  Independent peaks count:      18
print(f"Mean excess (above threshold): {mean_excess:.6f} (or {mean_excess:.2e})")
#  Mean excess (above threshold):  0.004166 (or 4.17e-03)
print(f"Median excess:                {median_excess:.6f} (or {median_excess:.2e})")
#  Median excess:                  0.002855 (or 2.85e-03)
print(f"Mean gap between peaks:       {mean_gap_days:.1f} days")
#  Mean gap between peaks:       38.3 days
print(f"Minimum gap between peaks:    {min_gap_days:.1f} days")
#  Minimum gap between peaks:    5.0 days
print(f"Daily exceedance rate (λ):    {freq_per_day:.4f} ({freq_per_day*100:.2f}%)")
#  Daily exceedance rate (λ):   0.0347 (3.47%)

# ==============================================================================
# 4. Tail heaviness estimation (Xi) (Generalized Pareto Distribution)
# ==============================================================================

# GPD fit fixing loc=0 for the excesses
shape_xi, loc, scale_gpd = genpareto.fit(exceedances, floc=0)

print("\n===== TAIL HEAVINESS (GPD FIT) =====")
print(f"GPD Threshold (u):  {threshold_eva:.6f}")
#  GPD Threshold (u):  0.008497
print(f"GPD Scale (σ_gpd):   {scale_gpd:.6f}")
#  GPD Scale (σ_gpd):   0.004872
print(f"GPD Shape (ξ):       {shape_xi:.4f}")
#  GPD Shape (ξ):      -0.1630

threshold_eva = 0.008497;
scale_gpd = 0.004872;
shape_xi = -0.1630;

jump_endpoint = threshold_eva - scale_gpd / shape_xi
print(f"Jump endpoint (%): {jump_endpoint * 100:.4f}%")
#  Jump endpoint (%): 3.8387%

if shape_xi > 0.5:
    print("Diagnosis: Extremely heavy tail.")
    print("Variance is theoretically infinite.")
elif shape_xi > 0:
    print("Diagnosis: Heavy-tailed Fréchet regime.")
    print("Power-law type tail.")
elif abs(shape_xi) < 1e-6:
    print("Diagnosis: Expocurrential-type tail.")
    print("GPD limit as xi -> 0.")
else:
    print("Diagnosis: Bounded Weibull-type tail.")
    print("The distribution has a finite upper endpoint.")

#  Diagnosis: Bounded Weibull-type tail.
#  The distribution has a finite upper endpoint

# ==============================================================================
# 5. SPIKE-AND-SLAB VARIANCES
# ==============================================================================

# Baseline noise (without the jump)
baseline_vol = returns[abs_returns <= threshold_eva]

spike_prob = 1.0 - freq_per_day
spike_var = np.var(baseline_vol)
slab_var = np.var(exceedances)

sigma_spike = np.sqrt(spike_var)
sigma_slab = np.sqrt(slab_var)

print("\n===== RECOMMENDED SPIKE-AND-SLAB PRIORS =====")
print(f"Spike Probability (π_spike): {spike_prob:.4f} ({spike_prob*100:.2f}%)")
#  Spike Probability (π_spike): 0.9653 (96.53%)
print(f"Jump Probability (p_jump):   {freq_per_day:.4f} ({freq_per_day*100:.2f}%)")
#  Jump Probability (p_jump):    0.0347 (3.47%)
print(f"Spike SD (σ_spike):          {sigma_spike:.6f}")
#  Spike SD (σ_spike):          0.003451
print(f"Slab SD (σ_slab):            {sigma_slab:.6f}")
#  Slab SD (σ_slab):            0.003847

jump_probability = 0.0347

#  "Events exceeding 0.008497%(u) are relatively rare (~3.47% after declustering), 
#   and the estimated tail is bounded, with an endpoint at approximately 3.8387%."

# ==============================================================================
# 7. PLOTTING
# ==============================================================================

plt.figure(figsize=(12, 5))
plt.plot(returns.index, returns, label='Fractional Difference Returns', alpha=0.5, color='blue')
plt.axhline(threshold_eva, color='red', linestyle='--', label=f'Threshold (95th %ile = {threshold_eva:.5f})')
plt.scatter(extremes.index, extremes, color='darkorange', edgecolors='black', zorder=5, label='Declustered Extremes (POT)')

plt.title("EUR/USD EVA Framework: Declustered Peaks Over Threshold", fontsize=12)
plt.ylabel("Fractional Return")
plt.legend(loc='upper left')
plt.grid(True, linestyle=':', alpha=0.6)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()

# ==========================================
# 1. Trend Indicators and Moving Averages
# ==========================================
ema_fast = ta.trend.EMAIndicator(close=EURUSD["Close"], window=20).ema_indicator()
ema_medium = ta.trend.EMAIndicator(close=EURUSD["Close"], window=50).ema_indicator()
ema_slow = ta.trend.EMAIndicator(close=EURUSD["Close"], window=200).ema_indicator()

# Distâncias percentuais em vez de valores brutos (Garante Estacionariedade)
dist_ema_fast = (EURUSD["Close"] - ema_fast) / EURUSD["Close"]
dist_ema_medium = (EURUSD["Close"] - ema_medium) / EURUSD["Close"]
dist_ema_slow = (EURUSD["Close"] - ema_slow) / EURUSD["Close"]

# ==========================================
# 2. Volatility Indicator (ATR)
# ==========================================

def calculate_atr(df, period=14):
  high = df["High"]
  low = df["Low"]
  close = df["Close"]
  tr1 = high - low
  tr2 = (high - close.shift(1)).abs()
  tr3 = (low - close.shift(1)).abs()
  true_range = pd.concat([tr1, tr2, tr3], axis=1).max(axis=1)
  return true_range.ewm(alpha=1 / period, adjust=False).mean()

ATR = calculate_atr(EURUSD, period=14)

# ==========================================
# 3. Adaptive (Rolling) Support and Resistance
# ==========================================
# Simple rolling support and resistance based on 20-period lows/highs to avoid data leakage.
rolling_support = EURUSD["Low"].rolling(window=20).min()
rolling_resistance = EURUSD["High"].rolling(window=20).max()

# ==========================================
# 4. Directional and Momentum Indicators
# ==========================================
rsi = ta.momentum.rsi(EURUSD["Close"], window=14)

# Cumulative Volume Delta (CVD)
EURUSD["Direction"] = np.where(EURUSD["Close"] >= EURUSD["Open"], 1, -1)
EURUSD["Delta"] = EURUSD["volume_proxy"] * EURUSD["Direction"]
EURUSD["CVD"] = EURUSD["Delta"].cumsum()

macd_indicator = ta.trend.MACD(
    EURUSD["Close"], window_fast=12, window_slow=26, window_sign=9
)
MACD_Line = macd_indicator.macd()
MACD_Histogram = macd_indicator.macd_diff()

adx_indicator = ta.trend.ADXIndicator(
    high=EURUSD["High"], low=EURUSD["Low"], close=EURUSD["Close"], window=14
)
ADX = adx_indicator.adx().fillna(0)

# ==========================================
# 5. Daily Order-Flow Proxy (DOFP)
# ==========================================
eps = 1e-12

# CLV (Close Location Value)
clv = (
    (2.0 * EURUSD["Close"] - EURUSD["High"] - EURUSD["Low"])
    / (EURUSD["High"] - EURUSD["Low"] + eps)
).clip(-1, 1)

# Proxy Volume Pressure
volume_proxy = (
    (EURUSD["High"] - EURUSD["Low"]) / (EURUSD["Close"] + eps)
).astype(float)
pressure = clv * (0.5 + 0.5 * volume_proxy)

# Fractional Return / Normalized Volatility (Preserving Pandas Series)
# Assuming you have lagged returns

returns_frac = EURUSD["Close"].pct_change().fillna(0)
relativity_volatility = ATR / EURUSD["Close"]

direction_volatility = pd.Series(
    np.tanh(returns_frac / (relativity_volatility + eps)), index=EURUSD.index
)

adx_strength = (ADX / 100.0).clip(0.0, 1.0)
trend_pressure = direction_volatility * adx_strength

# DOFP Raw & Saturation 
dofp_raw = 0.55 * pressure + 0.45 * trend_pressure
dofp = pd.Series(np.tanh(dofp_raw), index=EURUSD.index)

# ==========================================
# 6. Price and Average Event Dummies
# ==========================================

delta = 0.001
D_breakout = (
    (EURUSD["Close"] > rolling_resistance * (1 + delta))
    | (EURUSD["Close"] < rolling_support * (1 - delta))
).astype(int)

epsilon = 0.002
dist_support = (EURUSD["Close"] - rolling_support).abs() / rolling_support
dist_resistance = (
    EURUSD["Close"] - rolling_resistance
).abs() / rolling_resistance
D_near_sr = (
    (dist_support < epsilon) | (dist_resistance < epsilon)
).astype(int)

# EMA Pressure Dummy
ema_spread = (ema_fast - ema_slow) / EURUSD["Close"]
threshold = ema_spread.abs().rolling(200).quantile(0.1)
D_ema_pressure = (ema_spread.abs() < threshold).astype(int)

# ==========================================
# 7. EMA volatility proxy by compression
# ==========================================

ema_signal = ((ema_fast - ema_medium) + (ema_medium - ema_fast)) / EURUSD["Close"]
ema_signal_normalized = pd.Series(
    np.tanh((ema_signal - ema_signal.mean()) / (ema_signal.std() + eps)),
    index=EURUSD.index,
)

ema_compression = (abs(ema_fast - ema_medium) + abs(ema_medium - ema_slow)) / EURUSD["Close"]
ema_compression = pd.Series(
    np.tanh(
        (ema_compression - ema_compression.mean())
        / (ema_compression.std() + eps)
    ),
    index=EURUSD.index,
)

# ==========================================
# 8. Final Feature Matrix
# ==========================================
features = pd.concat(
    [
        ema_fast,
        ema_medium,
        ema_slow,
        ATR,
        rsi,
		EURUSD['CVD'],
        MACD_Line,
        MACD_Histogram,
        ADX,
        dofp,
        D_breakout,
        D_near_sr,
        D_ema_pressure,
        ema_signal_normalized,
        ema_compression,
    ],
    axis=1,
)

features.columns = [
    "ema_fast",
    "ema_medium",
    "ema_slow",
    "ATR",
    "rsi",
	"CVD",
    "MACD_Line",
    "MACD_Histogram",
    "ADX",
    "dofp",
    "D_breakout",
    "D_near_sr",
    "D_ema_pressure",
    "ema_signal",
    "ema_compression",
]

features = features.fillna(0)

pd.DataFrame(features).to_csv (r'C:\Users\Rafael Xavier Botelho\Documents\FOREX_EURSUD\features.csv', index=None)

# ==========================================================
# Pivot Detection
# ==========================================================

def detect_pivots(price, window=5):
    """
    Detect swing highs and swing lows in a price series.

    A pivot is confirmed when a price is the minimum or maximum
    within a local window of observations.

    Parameters
    ========--
    price : array-like
        Price time series.

    window : int
        Number of candles before and after the point used to
        confirm a pivot.

    Returns
    ========
    pivot_low : list
        List of (index, price) tuples representing swing lows.

    pivot_high : list
        List of (index, price) tuples representing swing highs.
    """
    pivot_low = []
    pivot_high = []
    for i in range(window, len(price) - window):
        # extract local window
        segment = price[i-window:i+window+1]
        # detect swing low
        if price[i] == segment.min():
            pivot_low.append((i, price[i]))
        # detect swing high
        if price[i] == segment.max():
            pivot_high.append((i, price[i]))
    return pivot_low, pivot_high


# ==========================================================
# Liquidity Heatmap
# ==========================================================

def liquidity_heatmap(price_grid, pivots, bandwidth=0.0005):
    """
    Estimate liquidity density across price levels.

    Each pivot is interpreted as a potential liquidity pool
    (stops, institutional limits, or resting orders).

    A Gaussian kernel is used to spread liquidity around
    the pivot price.

    Parameters
    ========--
    price_grid : array
        Grid of price levels where liquidity will be estimated.

    pivots : list
        List of pivot points.

    bandwidth : float
        Width of liquidity cluster around pivots.

    Returns
    ========
    heatmap : array
        Liquidity density across price levels.
    """
    heatmap = np.zeros_like(price_grid)
    for idx, p in pivots:
        # Gaussian liquidity distribution around pivot
        heatmap += np.exp(-(price_grid - p)**2 / (2 * bandwidth**2))
    return heatmap


# ==========================================================
# Adaptive Institutional Trendline
# ==========================================================

def adaptive_trendline(points):
    """
    Fit robust institutional trendlines (LTA / LTB).

    Uses RANSAC regression to avoid outliers and
    manipulation spikes.

    Parameters
    ========--
    points : list
        List of pivot points.

    Returns
    ========
    slope : float
    intercept : float
    """
    x = np.array([p[0] for p in points]).reshape(-1,1)
    y = np.array([p[1] for p in points])
    ransac = RANSACRegressor(LinearRegression())
    ransac.fit(x,y)
    slope = ransac.estimator_.coef_[0]
    intercept = ransac.estimator_.intercept_
    return slope, intercept


# ==========================================================
# Liquidity Heatmap Pipeline
# ==========================================================

def compute_liquidity_heatmap(price, grid_size=None, smooth_sigma=3):
    """
    Full pipeline to compute liquidity heatmap from price series.

    Steps
    ====-
    1. Detect pivots
    2. Build price grid
    3. Estimate liquidity density
    4. Smooth liquidity clusters

    Parameters
    ========--
    price : array
        Price time series.

    grid_size : int
        Number of price levels in the grid.

    smooth_sigma : float
        Gaussian smoothing parameter.

    Returns
    ========
    price_grid : array
        Grid of price levels.

    heatmap_smooth : array
        Smoothed liquidity density.

    pivots : list
        All detected pivots.
    """
    # detect pivots
    pivot_low, pivot_high = detect_pivots(price)
    pivots = pivot_low + pivot_high
    # build price grid
    price_grid = np.linspace(price.min(), price.max(), grid_size)
    # compute liquidity density
    heatmap = liquidity_heatmap(price_grid, pivots)
    # smooth clusters (institutional zones are not single prices)
    heatmap_smooth = gaussian_filter(heatmap, sigma=smooth_sigma)
    return price_grid, heatmap_smooth, pivots
	
grid_size = len(EURUSD[["Close"]].values.flatten().astype(np.float64))	

price_grid, heatmap, pivots = compute_liquidity_heatmap(EURUSD[["Close"]].values.flatten().astype(np.float64)
                              , grid_size)
							  							 
gradient = np.gradient(heatmap, price_grid)
liquidity_grad_t = np.interp(EURUSD[["Close"]].values.flatten().astype(np.float64), price_grid, gradient)

# temporal smoothing
liquidity_grad_t = gaussian_filter1d(liquidity_grad_t, sigma=4)

# robust normalization
median = np.median(liquidity_grad_t)
mad = np.median(np.abs(liquidity_grad_t - median))
liquidity_grad_t = (liquidity_grad_t - median) / (mad + 1e-8)

# saturation
liquidity_grad_t = np.tanh(liquidity_grad_t)

plt.plot(liquidity_grad_t)
plt.title("Liquidity Gradient Signal")
plt.show()

# Figure1.1l Liquidity Gradient Signal

#    This plot shows the time evolution of the normalized liquidity gradient, 
#    which measures the directional imbalance of liquidity around the current price.
#    Values close to +1 indicate that liquidity is concentrated above the price level,
#    suggesting potential upward pressure as the market moves toward higher liquidity pools
#    (such as clustered stop orders or resting limits). Conversely, values near −1 indicate 
#    liquidity concentrated below the price, implying potential downward pressure. 
#    The frequent oscillations between positive and negative values indicate that the market
#    is continuously transitioning between nearby liquidity zones, which is typical of short-horizon 
#    microstructure dynamics where price discovery occurs through repeated interactions with local 
#    liquidity clusters.

pd.DataFrame(liquidity_grad_t).to_csv (r'C:\Users\Rafael Xavier Botelho\Documents\FOREX_EURSUD\liquidity_grad_t.csv', index=None)
liquidity_grad_t = pd.read_csv("C:/Users/Rafael Xavier Botelho/Documents/FOREX_EURSUD/liquidity_grad_t.csv")

def liquidity_at_price(price_series, price_grid, heatmap):
    """
    Map price series to liquidity density using interpolation.

    Parameters
    ========--
    price_series : array
        Time series of prices.

    price_grid : array
        Grid of price levels used in the heatmap.

    heatmap : array
        Liquidity density across price grid.

    Returns
    ========
    liquidity_t : array
        Liquidity density evaluated at each time t.
    """
    liquidity_t = np.interp(price_series, price_grid, heatmap)
    return liquidity_t
	
liquidity_t = liquidity_at_price(EURUSD[["Close"]].values.flatten().astype(np.float64), price_grid, heatmap)
scalp_index = liquidity_t / (relativity_volatility.values.flatten().astype(np.float64) + 1e-8)	

# temporal smoothing
scalp_index_smoothed = gaussian_filter1d(scalp_index, sigma=4)

# robust normalization
median = np.median(scalp_index_smoothed)
mad = np.median(np.abs(scalp_index_smoothed - median))
scalp_index_smoothed = (scalp_index_smoothed - median) / (mad + 1e-8)

# saturation
scalp_index_smoothed = np.tanh(scalp_index_smoothed)

plt.plot(scalp_index_smoothed)
plt.title("Liquidity versus Price Movement")
plt.show() 

# Figure_1.1m Liquidity versus Price Movement - scalp_index

#   This plot compares the normalized liquidity gradient with the price movement over time.
#   The two curves closely track each other, suggesting a strong relationship between the direction
#   of the liquidity gradient and subsequent price changes. When the liquidity gradient becomes positive,
#   price movements tend to follow upward, while negative gradients tend to coincide with downward price
#   movements. This alignment indicates that the estimated liquidity field captures an important 
#   compocurrent of market microstructure, where prices tend to move toward regions with higher liquidity density, 
#   reflecting the influence of stop clusters, institutional orders, and liquidity-seeking trading behavior.

pd.DataFrame(scalp_index_smoothed).to_csv (r'C:\Users\Rafael Xavier Botelho\Documents\FOREX_EURSUD\scalp_index_smoothed.csv', index=None)

###################################    
#                                 #     
#   Spatial Selection of Support  #
#      and Resistance Levels      #     
#                                 #      
###################################

def evaluate_market_levels(df_data, resistances_list, supports_list):
  current_price = df_data['Close'].iloc[-1]
  # --- NEAREST RESISTANCE (Just ABOVE the current price) ---
  valid_resistances = [r for r in resistances_list if r['mid'] > current_price]
  if valid_resistances:
    nearest_res = min(valid_resistances, key=lambda z: z['mid'])
    dist_pct = (nearest_res['mid'] - current_price) / current_price
    prox = (
        'Immediate'
        if dist_pct < 0.002
        else (
            'Near Term'
            if dist_pct < 0.005
            else ('Moderate' if dist_pct < 0.01 else 'Distant')
        )
    )
    touches = nearest_res.get('touches', 1)
    strg = 'Strong' if touches >= 3 else ('Moderate' if touches == 2 else 'Weak')
    nearest_res['class'] = f'{strg} ({prox})'
    print('--- IMMEDIATE RESISTANCE ---')
    print(f"Range: {nearest_res['low']:.4f} - {nearest_res['high']:.4f}")
    print(
        f"Mid Level: {nearest_res['mid']:.4f} | Classification:"
        f" {nearest_res['class']} ({touches} touches)"
    )
  else:
    print('No valid resistance found above current price.')
  # --- SUPORTE MAIS PRÓXIMO (Apenas ABAIXO do preço atual) ---
  valid_supports = [s for s in supports_list if s['mid'] < current_price]
  if valid_supports:
    nearest_sup = max(valid_supports, key=lambda z: z['mid'])
    dist_pct = (current_price - nearest_sup['mid']) / current_price
    prox = (
        'Immediate'
        if dist_pct < 0.002
        else (
            'Near Term'
            if dist_pct < 0.005
            else ('Moderate' if dist_pct < 0.01 else 'Distant')
        )
    )
    touches = nearest_sup.get('touches', 1)
    strg = 'Strong' if touches >= 3 else ('Moderate' if touches == 2 else 'Weak')
    nearest_sup['class'] = f'{strg} ({prox})'
    print('\n--- IMMEDIATE SUPPORT ---')
    print(f"Range: {nearest_sup['low']:.4f} - {nearest_sup['high']:.4f}")
    print(
        f"Mid Level: {nearest_sup['mid']:.4f} | Classification:"
        f" {nearest_sup['class']} ({touches} touches)"
    )
  else:
    print('No valid support found below current price.')
	
evaluate_market_levels(EURUSD, resistances, supports)	

#   --- IMMEDIATE RESISTANCE ---
#   Range: 1.1468 - 1.1488
#   Mid Level: 1.1479 | Classification: Weak (Immediate) (1 touches)

#   --- IMMEDIATE SUPPORT ---
#   Range: 1.1389 - 1.1410
#   Mid Level: 1.1403 | Classification: Weak (Moderate) (1 touches)

current_close = df['close'].iloc[-1]

# 1. Nearest Institutional Support (Below Current Close)
if inst_supports:
    closest_inst_support = max(inst_supports, key=lambda x: x['mid'])['mid']
else:
    closest_inst_support = current_close - 0.0100  # Fallback generoso (100 pips)

# 2. Nearest Institutional Resistance (Above Current Close)
if inst_resistances:
    closest_inst_resistance = min(inst_resistances, key=lambda x: x['mid'])['mid']
else:
    closest_inst_resistance = current_close + 0.0100  # Fallback generoso (100 pips)

print(f"\nCurrent Close: {current_close:.5f}")
print(f"Nearest Inst Support    : {closest_inst_support:.5f}")
# Nearest Inst Support    : 1.14030
print(f"Nearest Inst Resistance : {closest_inst_resistance:.5f}")
# Nearest Inst Resistance : 1.14793


def classify_key_level_type(price, pip_tolerance=0.0010):
    """
    Classifies a price level as a Big Figure (Major) or a Secondary Key Level (Minor).
    
    - Big Figure: Ends in .0000 or .0050 (1.1400, 1.1450, 1.1500)
    - Secondary Key Level: Ends in .0025, .0075, or multiples of 20/80 pips (1.1425, 1.1475, 1.1480)
    """
    # 1. Finds the nearest Big Figure (50-pip step = 0.0050)
    big_figure = np.round(price / 0.0050) * 0.0050
    dist_to_big_figure = abs(price - big_figure)
    
    # 2.Find the nearest secondary level (step of 25 pips = 0.0025)
    sec_level = np.round(price / 0.0025) * 0.0025
    dist_to_sec_level = abs(price - sec_level)
    
    if dist_to_big_figure <= pip_tolerance:
        return 'BIG_FIGURE', big_figure
    elif dist_to_sec_level <= pip_tolerance:
        return 'SECONDARY_KEY_LEVEL', sec_level
    else:
        return 'STANDARD_ZONE', price

sup_type, sup_ref = classify_key_level_type(closest_inst_support)
res_type, res_ref = classify_key_level_type(closest_inst_resistance)

print(f"Support {closest_inst_support:.5f}   -> Tipo: {sup_type} (Ref: {sup_ref:.5f})")
#  Support 1.14030   -> Tipo: BIG_FIGURE (Ref: 1.14000)
print(f"Resistence  {closest_inst_resistance:.5f} -> Tipo: {res_type} (Ref: {res_ref:.5f})")
#  Resistence  1.14793 -> Tipo: SECONDARY_KEY_LEVEL (Ref: 1.14750)


################*/           *\ ################
# 
#               ==== Passage 2 ====
#       Modelling and extrapolation inference .
#
################*/           *\ ################

GBM_MS_SV_AR2_MomentumLSTM = """

/*           
                                                                 Abstract 

         We introduce a novel Bayesian stochastic volatility (SV) model that integrates Markov-switching regimes, a non-linear state-dependent Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) mean-reversion mechanism, a Quasi-Momentum Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) architecture,
		 and a hybrid Price Action/Smart Money Concepts (SMC) decision framework to capture complex, time-varying financial dynamics. Our model features an AR(2) process for log-volatility, augmented with momentum-driven dynamics and a threshold-gated
		 OU drift that models power-law mean-reverting behavior during specific structural regimes. An underlying Hidden Markov Model (HMM) governs unobserved regime transitions, allowing for distinct market states with regime-dependent parameters. 
		 Crucially, the framework dynamically estimates state-dependent volatility half-life metrics, offering an interpretable decay measure for empirical shocks. Structurally, unobserved asset prices follow a continuous-time Geometric Brownian Motion (GBM) 
		 with deterministic drift and jump-diffusion components, discretized via an Euler-Maruyama SDE approximation. Furthermore, we project the latent Student-t predictive distributions into a tiered, rule-governed signal hierarchy. At the primary deterministic level,
		 high-conviction institutional triggers—specifically Liquidity Sweeps, multi-touch Big Figure exhaustion counts, and Order Block mitigations coupled with strong volume Displacement—override baseline forecasts to enforce sharp directional reversals.
		 In the absence of primary triggers, the model falls back on a continuous logit-space decision layer, dynamically weighting directional order-flow proxy metrics (CVD and RSI confluences) alongside a quantitative Price Action scoring system (encompassing Doji geometries and Fair Value Gaps). 
		 
		 Implemented with Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) via Stan, this comprehensive Bayesian framework provides robust inference of latent volatility dynamics and market microstructure signals, significantly enhancing directional risk characterization, signal quality, and financial forecasting.								  
																  
																  
																  Resumo

         Apresentamos um novo modelo bayesiano de volatilidade estocástica (SV) que integra regimes de mudança de Markov (Markov-switching), um mecanismo de reversão à média de Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) não linear e dependente do estado, uma arquitetura LSTM (Long Short-Term Memory) com quase-momentum,
		 e uma estrutura de decisão híbrida que combina *Price Action* e *Smart Money Concepts* (SMC) para capturar dinâmicas financeiras complexas e variáveis ​​no tempo. Nosso modelo utiliza um processo AR(2) para a log-volatilidade, complementado por dinâmicas impulsionadas por momentum e um termo de deriva (drift) OU
		 controlado por limiares (*threshold-gated*), que modela o comportamento de reversão à média seguindo uma lei de potência durante regimes estruturais específicos. Um Modelo Oculto de Markov (HMM) subjacente rege as transições de regime não observadas, permitindo a existência de estados de mercado distintos com parâmetros dependentes do regime.
		 Fundamentalmente, a estrutura estima dinamicamente métricas de meia-vida da volatilidade dependentes do estado, oferecendo uma medida de decaimento interpretável para choques empíricos. Estruturalmente, os preços dos ativos não observados seguem um Movimento Browniano Geométrico (GBM) em tempo contínuo,
		 com componentes de deriva determinística e de salto-difusão (*jump-diffusion*), discretizados por meio de uma aproximação de SDE de Euler-Maruyama. Além disso, projetamos as distribuições preditivas latentes (do tipo t-Student) em uma hierarquia de sinais em níveis, regida por regras. No nível determinístico primário,
		 gatilhos institucionais de alta convicção — especificamente varreduras de liquidez (*Liquidity Sweeps*), contagens de exaustão em níveis de preço redondo (*Big Figure*) com múltiplos toques e mitigações de blocos de ordens (*Order Blocks*) associadas a forte deslocamento de preço (*Displacement*) com volume elevado — prevalecem sobre as previsões de referência para impor reversões direcionais acentuadas.
		 Na ausência de gatilhos primários, o modelo recorre a uma camada de decisão contínua no espaço *logit*, ponderando dinamicamente métricas *proxy* de fluxo de ordens direcional (confluências de CVD e RSI) juntamente com um sistema quantitativo de pontuação de *Price Action* (abrangendo geometrias Doji e *Fair Value Gaps*).
		 
		 Implementada com o método Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) via Stan, essa estrutura bayesiana abrangente fornece uma inferência robusta das dinâmicas latentes de volatilidade e dos sinais de microestrutura de mercado, aprimorando significativamente a caracterização do risco direcional, a qualidade dos sinais e a previsão financeira.

            YU, J.; MEYER, R. Multivariate stochastic volatility models: Bayesian estimation and model comparison.

functions{

 real student_t_quantile(real p, real nu) {

    // wide initial limits
    real lower = -50;
    real upper = 50;
    real mid;
    real cdf_mid;

    // tolerance
    real tol = 1e-8;

    // safety
    if (p <= 0) return negative_infinity();
    if (p >= 1) return positive_infinity();

    // bisection
    while (upper - lower > tol) {

      mid = 0.5 * (lower + upper);
      cdf_mid = student_t_cdf(mid, nu, 0, 1);

      if (cdf_mid > p)
        upper = mid;
      else
        lower = mid;
    }

    return 0.5 * (lower + upper);
  }
  
 real expected_shortfall_t(
    real mu,
    real sigma,
    real nu,
    real alpha   // ex: 0.95 ou 0.05
  ) {
    // Calculation of Expected Shortfall (ES) for Dynamic Stop Loss
    // It reflects the average losses in the tail, making it more reliable than simple VaR.
		
    // standardized t-quantile
    real q = student_t_quantile(alpha, nu);

    // quantile density
    real pdf_q = exp(student_t_lpdf(q | nu, 0, 1));

    // Standardized ES
    real es_std = (nu + square(q)) / (nu - 1)  * pdf_q;

    // Scaled ES 
    if (alpha < 0.5)
        return mu - sigma * es_std / alpha;
    else
        return mu + sigma * es_std / (1 - alpha);
    }
}

   // Number of observed data points (time series length)
   int<lower=1> N;
   // Candlestick pivot radius for fibonacci calculations
   int<lower=1> M;         
   vector[N] y;             // Observed prices (Close) 
   vector[N] open;          // Opening prices (Required for Price Action Wick Ratios)
   vector[N] high;          // High prices (Required for Price Action Wick Ratios)   
   vector[N] low;           // Low prices (Required for Price Action Wick Ratios)
   vector[N] rv;  // Parkimson  volatility proxy
   real dt;       // EURUSD D1 Close Prices
   real eva_mean; // Theoretical mean of the jump compocurrent derived from EVA (GPD expected value)
   real eva_sd;   // Theoretical standard deviation of the jump compocurrent derived from EVA
   // TP & SL levels 
   real farthest_resistance;   
   real farthest_support; 
   real big_figure_resistence;
   real big_figure_support;   
   //  Degrees of freedom t-student likelihood
   real nu;
   int<lower=0,upper=1> date[N, 5];
   // Price action indicators
   vector[N] D_breakout;
   vector[N] D_near_sr;
